{\\"path\\": \\"Necessary Assumption.gdoc\\", \\"modified\\": \\"2025-08-19T04:59:14.773000+00:00\\", \\"word_count\\": 2, \\"doc_id\\": \\"17Uz-X_s0e6lPfOcZMkuxoPmpC87To4Pu44Vyh49B7vs\\"}\\r\\nsubject: Megadoc Notes Necessary Assumption\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: LSAT_Overview_Concepts_Final.docx\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Assumption/Notes/LSAT_Overview_Concepts_Final.docx  **Modified:** 2025-05-16T00:37:08+00:00  **Words:** 876  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nLSAT Key Concepts Overview 1. Necessary Assumption (NA) • Definition: A necessary assumption is an unstated belief that is essential for the argument to remain valid. It bridges the gap between the premises and the conclusion, ensuring the argument remains logically sound. Without it, the argument collapses or fails to logically support the conclusion. • Purpose: It connects the premises and the conclusion. While it cannot by itself prove the conclusion, its truth is required for the argument to\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Necessary_Assumption Mastery.docx\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Assumption/Notes/Necessary_Assumption Mastery.docx  **Modified:** 2025-07-18T02:44:37.741000+00:00  **Words:** 804  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nNecessary Assumption Mastery Complete each stage in order. Score at least 4 of 5 before advancing. Stage n 1 Š Core Strategy & Basics Think like a mediator Š state what the author implies without judging. A necessary assumption is an unstated idea *required* for the conclusion. Roles: *Linking* (fills a gap) and *Defender* (rules out an objection). Negate the answer: keep subject, deny predicate; the conclusion should fail. Keywords: assumption, presupposes, depends, relies on. PT n 72 n S2 n Q1\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Necessary_Assumption_Mastery_Final.docx\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Assumption/Notes/Necessary_Assumption_Mastery_Final.docx  **Modified:** 2025-07-18T02:44:43.214000+00:00  **Words:** 780  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nNecessary Assumption Mastery Complete each stage in order. Score at least 4 of 5 before advancing. For every question: • **Conclusion** — write one concise line. • **Objection** — design a concrete scenario showing the conclusion isn’t guaranteed. • **Negation of Correct Answer** — write the correct answer in its negated form. • **Assumption Type** — mark Link or Defender. Stage 1 — Core Strategy & Basics — Think like a mediator — state what the author implies without judging. — A necessary assu\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Necessary_Assumption_Mastery_FinalV2_clean.docx\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Assumption/Notes/Necessary_Assumption_Mastery_FinalV2_clean.docx  **Modified:** 2025-07-18T02:44:36.075000+00:00  **Words:** 691  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nNecessary Assumption Mastery Complete each stage in order. Score at least 4 of 5 before advancing. Stage 1 Core Strategy & Basics Think like a mediator state what the author implies without judging. A necessary assumption is an unstated idea *required* for the conclusion. Roles: *Linking* (fills a gap) and *Defender* (rules out an objection). Negate the answer: keep subject, deny predicate; the conclusion should fail. Keywords: assumption, presupposes, depends, relies on. [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"12b455","wall_time_seconds":0.323655,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":31,"output":"corpus/Curriculum\\\\Flaw and Famous Flaws\\\\_HANDOFF\\\\README.md\\r\\ncorpus/Curriculum\\\\Main Conclusion\\\\_export_2026-04-29\\\\README.md\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"02c8a8","wall_time_seconds":0.2167031,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1179,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: teaching-notes\\r\\nsubject: Pop Quiz Assumption vs. Objection\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Pop Quiz: Assumption vs. Objection\\r\\n\\r\\n## Part 1 — Trigger Phrases\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each trigger phrase, identify whether it signals an **Assumption** or an **Objection**, then state what to do with the variable.\\r\\n\\r\\n| # | Trigger Phrase | Identify (Circle) | What Do We Do? |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 1 | Takes for granted that X | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 2 | Fails to consider that Y | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 3 | Presumes that X | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 4 | Overlooks the possibility that Y | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 5 | Ignores the possibility that Y | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 6 | Assumes that X | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 7 | Fails to establish that X | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 8 | Neglects to consider that Y | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Part 2 — Real Answer Choices\\r\\n\\r\\n| # | Answer Choice | Identify | What Do We Do? |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 9 | \\"The author fails to consider that there are probably species in existence that have not yet been identified.\\" | *[Example]* Objection | *If it is **TRUE** that there are probably species not yet identified, would that weaken the conclusion?* |\\r\\n| 10 | \\"Ignores the possibility that the bottle contained an acidic liquid other than vinegar.\\" | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 11 | \\"Assumes that the fact of a labeling error is proof of an intention to deceive.\\" | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 12 | \\"Ignores the possibility that some food additives are harmful to most people.\\" | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 13 | \\"Presumes that most consumers heed the warning labels on beverage containers.\\" | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 14 | \\"Fails to consider that the economy might not improve.\\" | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 15 | \\"Ignores the possibility that the city council is not the only body that can make such decisions.\\" | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n| 16 | \\"Assumes that the economy will improve in the near future.\\" | Assumption / Objection | __________ |\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Full Answer Key\\r\\n\\r\\n## Part 1: Generic Drill Key\\r\\n\\r\\n| # | Trigger Phrase | Type | What Do We Do? (The Correct Test) |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 1 | Takes for granted that X | **Assumption** | Make X **FALSE**. Does the argument crash? |\\r\\n| 2 | Fails to consider that Y | **Objection** | Take Y to be **TRUE**. Does the conclusion die? |\\r\\n| 3 | Presumes that X | **Assumption** | Make X **FALSE**. Does the argument crash? |\\r\\n| 4 | Overlooks the possibility that Y | **Objection** | Take Y to be **TRUE**. Does the conclusion die? |\\r\\n| 5 | Ignores the possibility that Y | **Objection** | Take Y to be **TRUE**. Does the conclusion die? |\\r\\n| 6 | Assumes that X | **Assumption** | Make X **FALSE**. Does the argument crash? |\\r\\n| 7 | Fails to establish that X | **Assumption** | Make X **FALSE**. Does the argument crash? |\\r\\n| 8 | Neglects to consider that Y | **Objection** | Take Y to be **TRUE**. Does the conclusion die? |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Part 2: Real LSAT Answer Key\\r\\n\\r\\n| # | Type | What Do We Do? (The Correct Logic Check) |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| 9 | **Objection** | \\"If it is TRUE that there are probably species not yet identified, would that weaken the conclusion?\\" |\\r\\n| 10 | **Objection** | \\"If it is TRUE that the bottle contained an acidic liquid other than vinegar, would that weaken the conclusion?\\" |\\r\\n| 11 | **Assumption** | \\"If a labeling error is NOT proof of an intention to deceive, does the argument crash?\\" |\\r\\n| 12 | **Objection** | \\"If it is TRUE that some food additives are harmful to most people, would that weaken the conclusion?\\" |\\r\\n| 13 | **Assumption** | \\"If most consumers do NOT heed the warning labels, does the argument crash?\\" |\\r\\n| 14 | **Objection** | \\"If it is TRUE that the economy might not improve, would that weaken the conclusion?\\" |\\r\\n| 15 | **Objection** | \\"If it is TRUE that the city council is not the only body that can make such decisions, would that weaken the conclusion?\\" |\\r\\n| 16 | **Assumption** | \\"If the economy will NOT improve in the near future, does the argument crash?\\" |\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n*Source: Google Doc `1lVgT7E8QPfOuSfLYrck1AsdsiZZ-Iyk530VfUIxrMF0` (titled \\"Flaw.gdoc\\"). Categorized as **Flaw/Famous Flaws** primary (the quiz teaches the Assumption-vs-Objection lens that drives Flaw answer-choice selection); secondary **Necessary Assumption** (the \\"Assumption\\" branch is the negation test that defines Necessary Assumption answers). Likely also corresponds to \\"Send me just the full Pop Quiz and Answer Key. for....gdoc\\" — same Pop Quiz content.*\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"30321a","wall_time_seconds":0.2212301,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2113,"output":"Warning: truncated output (original token count: 2113)\\nTotal output lines: 65\\n\\n---\\r\\ntype: operating-layer\\r\\nsubject: Aspiring Attorneys the business\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Aspiring Attorneys — the business\\r\\n\\r\\nAuthored context — what the business is and how it runs. One authoritative home (moved from `wiki/entities/aspiring-attorneys.md`, 2026-07-17); the router points here. Source of the underlying answers: `aios/aios-intake.md` (Q1–Q7, 2026-07-16).\\r\\n\\r\\nNorth star: **automate everything around the sessions so Joshua only shows up and teaches.**\\r\\n\\r\\n## What is sold, to whom\\r\\n\\r\\n- Personalized 1-on-1 LSAT tutoring/coaching: individual sessions, and five-hour / ten-hour packages **with between-session coaching included**. All paid upfront — no package-balance model exists.\\r\\n- Standard post-consultation offers are a single session at **$45/hour**, a five-hour package at **$40/hour**, and a ten-hour package at **$35/hour**.\\r\\n- In the prospect-facing standard follow-up, show only those hourly rates. Do not display the five-hour or ten-hour package totals. The workflow still validates the underlying total charged by each payment link before scheduling and sending.\\r\\n- Label the single-session link `Schedule here`. Label the five-hour and ten-hour package links `Purchase here`.\\r\\n- Post-consultation emails present all three standard offers with a verified action link for each only when no special arrangement exists. A verified person-specific arrangement replaces the standard menu entirely; never mix bespoke terms with unrelated standard offers.\\r\\n- A special arrangement exists only when Joshua explicitly states it, including an explicit statement preserved in the consultation notes or email thread. Never infer a discount or bespoke offer from financial concerns, fee-waiver status, geography, historical links, or other circumstances.\\r\\n- The standard consultation follow-up time is **3:00 PM Toronto time on the next calendar day, including weekends and statutory holidays**, after confirming the consultation occurred, usable notes exist, no follow-up was already sent, and the prospect has not paid.\\r\\n- Consultation follow-ups are scheduled automatically and send at the standard time unless Joshua stops or changes them beforehand. This approval-by-silence rule applies only to the bounded consultation follow-up workflow.\\r\\n- Only one or two lines in a consultation follow-up are personalized from the notes. They prioritize the prospect\'s goal, test timeline, and central LSAT problem; scores appear only when central, and sensitive details are not repeated merely to demonstrate personalization. The lines must sound specific and human, not generic or AI-written.\\r\\n- Do not include a breathing-room line claiming Joshua waited a couple of days or gave the prospect time to think. The standard follow-up sends the next calendar day, and the email must describe timing truthfully.\\r\\n- If the prospect uses 7Sage and their 7Sage profile URL is not already available, the consultation follow-up asks them to send the URL.\\r\\n- The request must specifically ask for the URL copied from the 7Sage Analytics page. Accept only the canonical `https://7sage.com/u/usr_<id>/review` form as an analytics URL. Drill-builder links, settings pages, and login-token URLs are not substitutes; login-token URLs must be treated as credentials and rejected.\\r\\n- Place the conditional 7Sage Analytics request beside the LSAT U signup step near the end of the consultation follow-up. It is a locked conditional line, not one of the one or two AI-personalized recap lines.\\r\\n- Variant C in `email system/Post-Consultation Follow-Up Template.md` is the canonical consultation follow-up base when no special arrangement exists. Workflow automation may fill its bounded personalization, 7Sage, pricing, and verified-link fields, but should not invent a replacement sales structure.\\r\\n- Remove the application-form step from Variant C. Consultation follow-ups must not send Google Forms or `gformsapp.com` links; intake belongs inside LSAT U during onboarding.\\r\\n…113 tokens truncated…ed subject `Next steps + tutoring options`.\\r\\n- Confirmed payment, not an email reply, triggers onboarding. Standard payments resolve through Stripe for packages and Stripe-linked Cal.com for single sessions; a verified special arrangement uses its stated authority. Cancel any scheduled consultation follow-up if payment arrives before its send time.\\r\\n- Automatic consultation follow-ups fail closed unless the recipient, current prospect state, prices, and price-to-link mapping all verify. Reject placeholders, malformed URLs, form editor links, login-token URLs, self-addressed tests, and one payment link reused across distinct paid tiers without an explicit arrangement. Run validation before scheduling and again before sending.\\r\\n- Verified standard links, all three re-checked live against Stripe/Cal.com on 2026-09-08: single session, `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/session-link` at $45 for 70 minutes; five-hour package, `https://buy.stripe.com/4gMaEY05F3dc9x13NqdZ61x`, renders \\"5 hours — $200.00\\"; ten-hour package, `https://buy.stripe.com/3cI6oI2dN5lk4cHcjWdZ61O`, renders \\"10 hour reduced — $350.00\\". The ten-hour link is correct; the earlier note that no verified $350 link existed was stale. Historical ten-hour links that charge $500 must still not be used.\\r\\n- Students in the **United States and Canada**.\\r\\n- Also building **LSAT U** (lsatprep.study) — the online prep platform; code and product docs live in `projects/lsatu/`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Money and booking\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Stripe** — packages; **Cal.com (Stripe-linked)** — individual paid sessions and free consultations; **e-transfer** rare (Canada).\\r\\n- All sessions land on **Google Calendar** and are conducted on **Google Meet**.\\r\\n- Tracking today is mainly the Stripe dashboard. **Gap:** no single per-student view of payments and sessions-used vs purchased — Cal.com answers sessions-used; a mirror view belongs in `students/` as `*.generated.md`, never a Markdown ledger.\\r\\n- **Self-scheduling links are situational.** Send the appropriate booking link, instead of proposing times, when a student has paid for a session but has not scheduled it, needs to reschedule, or has paid for a package with sessions to book. Do not apply this as a blanket rule to every prospect or email.\\r\\n- **Billing is contextual.** Before drafting a billing or payment reply, reconstruct the specific person\'s payment source, purchase type, amount or package, relevant agreement, session history, and timeline from the student file and authoritative live systems. Different students can legitimately have different arrangements. Do not force a generic billing answer onto incomplete facts.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Channels\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Gmail (contact@aspiringattorneys.com)** is the main channel — consultations, student threads, follow-ups. The **LSAT U in-app inbox** (Supabase) is a growing second. WhatsApp/iMessage negligible. Solo operator — no team.\\r\\n- Google Meet generates **full transcripts + notes after every session**, arriving by Gmail, attached to the calendar event, and in Drive — the reliable ingest signal for `students/<name>/`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The weekly time-sucks (ranked)\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Post-session work** — notes, homework, assigning work, study schedules.\\r\\n2. **Session prep** — per-student, analytics-informed (7Sage + platforms). Target state: prep is \\"nearly mindless\\"; the system reviews everything and prepares the session.\\r\\n3. **Pre/post-consultation email workflow** — partly covered by the `consultation-*` skills.\\r\\n4. Conducting sessions — the one part Joshua keeps.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Known gaps\\r\\n\\r\\n- No centralized teaching-materials store (scattered, mostly Google Drive) → `corpus/` is the designated home.\\r\\n- No centralized per-student records → `students/` is the designated home.\\r\\n- No central tutoring-business tracker (students, sessions, homework, follow-up state) → future cadence build; mirrors from backends (live systems are authority, the brain mirrors).\\r\\n\\r\\nVoice for anything a student or prospect reads: `wiki/teaching-voice.md`.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"57403f","wall_time_seconds":0.2039449,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1094,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: teaching-notes\\r\\nsubject: Corpus LSAT teaching knowledge\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Corpus — LSAT teaching knowledge\\r\\n\\r\\nThe second knowledge system: Joshua\'s LSAT teaching knowledge, built incrementally on the Karpathy LLM-wiki pattern as materials are ingested (books + notes, LSAT Lab, 7Sage material, drills, explanations). Eventually integrates into LSAT U as platform feedstock.\\r\\n\\r\\nFills through supervised ingests, one source at a time, logged in [[../wiki/log|the wiki log]]. Raw materials land in `raw/` first (immutable); the corpus holds the synthesized, interlinked teaching pages.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Conventions\\r\\n\\r\\n- Pages are LLM-written and LLM-maintained; Joshua curates sources and directs emphasis.\\r\\n- Wikilink corpus concepts into `students/<name>/` pages when a student is working that area, and into LSAT U material as it ships.\\r\\n- Question-type taxonomy, method pages, and drill-design principles get their own pages as they accumulate — no empty scaffolding in advance.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pages\\r\\n\\r\\n### Teaching methods recovered from the old local vault (2026-10-07)\\r\\n\\r\\n- [Loophole teacher reference](loophole-online/index.md): translation, quipping, CLIR, question strategies and review routines, with attributed source navigation and defective-capture warnings.\\r\\n- [Diagnose the cause of a miss](tutoring-diagnosis.md): the recovered 7Sage four-category coaching framework and whole-section timing review, retained as a private teacher reference.\\r\\n- [Source and comparison](../wiki/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07.md): preservation hashes, full local-file coverage, missing images and historical-policy conflicts.\\r\\n\\r\\n### `lectures/` · read-aloud scripts and teaching visuals\\r\\n\\r\\n- [[lectures/index]] · LSAT Lab source transcripts, teleprompter scripts, initial concept boards, and worked-question visuals. Packages preserve source timestamps and explicit completeness checks.\\r\\n\\r\\n### `Curriculum/` — the LR curriculum (ingested 2026-07-17)\\r\\n\\r\\n118 markdown pages recovered from `raw/sources/lsat-curriculum/_stragglers/local-ai-gather/…/Curriculum/` — the corpus folder tree had been scaffolded from this source in the 2026-07-16 rebuild, but the files themselves were never copied. Each topic carries `Notes/` (the teaching) and `Homework/` (the assigned drills).\\r\\n\\r\\n| Topic | Pages | Topic | Pages |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Main Conclusion | 21 | Necessary Assumption | 7 |\\r\\n| Flaw and Famous Flaws | 11 | Conditional Logic | 7 |\\r\\n| Abstract | 6 | Causation | 6 |\\r\\n| Principle | 4 | Role | 4 |\\r\\n| Sufficient Assumption | 4 | Must be True | 3 |\\r\\n| Strengthen | 2 | Parallel | 2 |\\r\\n| Rebuttals | 2 | Comparison | 1 |\\r\\n| Method | 1 | Most Supported | 1 |\\r\\n| Parallel Flaw | 1 | | |\\r\\n\\r\\n- `Curriculum/_question-feature-index/` — 33 pages indexing question features across PTs 101–159 (famous-flaws, complex-conditionals, 2-speaker, science, except, …).\\r\\n- `Curriculum/_meta/lr-taxonomy.md` — the LR question-type taxonomy.\\r\\n- `Curriculum/LR/` — 2 homework pages exported live from Google Drive (pilot, 2026-07-17).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Known gaps:** topics scaffolded but still unfilled — Agree Disagree, Bootcamp, Match Family, Must Be False, Paradox, Quantifiers, Weaken, Reading Comprehension. Their source material is split between `raw/sources/lsat-curriculum/LSAT DUMP/` (many entries are stale `.gdoc` Drive pointers whose IDs no longer resolve) and Google Drive itself, where a past migration converted docs to PDFs/scans under new IDs. Recovering those needs a Drive-side pass, not a local copy.\\r\\n\\r\\n### `lr-explanations/` — models for writing LR explanations (2026-09-26)\\r\\n\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/golden-set]] — the 51 LSAT Lab explanations new LSAT U explanations are modelled on, with the quality rubric.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/models/index]] — model library: 334 top-tier existing explanations, 10 to 20 per LR type, with what the best ones do for each type.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/drafts/pilot-2026-09-27]] — 20-question pilot of new explanations, waiting for Joshua.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/golden-set-observations]] — strong-vs-loose comparison, step structure per type, voice notes.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/golden-set-candidates]] — all 80 candidates and the type x feature coverage grid.\\r\\n- [[lr-explanations/fixes/index]] — the fix list: one line per issue, with detail files beside it (revamp list, Choice Note issues).\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"174ba8","wall_time_seconds":0.3078336,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4583,"output":"\\r\\nFILE raw\\\\sources\\\\old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07\\\\Clippings\\\\loophole-online\\\\training-camp\\\\learn\\\\The Worst Conditional Habit Everyone Has • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"The Worst Conditional Habit Everyone Has • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/learn/video/the-worst-conditional-habit-everyone-has\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## The Worst Conditional Habit Everyone Has\\r\\n\\r\\n0:11\\r\\n\\r\\n/\\r\\n\\r\\n3:48\\r\\n\\r\\n- 0:00All right, the worst conditional habit everybody has. You\'re probably expecting it to be like some super technical, mathy sounding thing of like, uh, the consequence. It\'s like really not that.\\r\\n- 0:13The worst conditional habit everyone has is that they are very all or nothing about like an exclusive relationship between diagramming and understanding.\\r\\n- 0:23Very few people will actually combine a conceptual understanding of the stimulus\'s Translation with a diagramming level understanding. Typically if someone is going to diagram, they will want to skip straight to diagram and diagramming and not really think about what the sentence means in any kind\\r\\n- 0:41of conceptual way that they will kind of let the diagramming do that step for them. But here\'s the problem with that.\\r\\n- 0:47If you let the diagramming do the understanding step for you, then your diagram might end up wrong. Um, your diagram might not actually represent the real meaning of the sentence or phrase that you\'re trying to diagram.\\r\\n- 1:00And so then Xing out that moment of conceptual Translation before you diagram, it really just creates a very faulty picture on all but the most simple conditionals.\\r\\n- 1:13And very often, I think the reason why this happens is, you know, time, but beyond the, the time panic aspect of it is people will not know their conditional indicators typically well enough to be able to easily rephrase them into a conceptual Translation\\r\\n- 1:30that translating a sentence that contains the word only if into a personalized if then feels harder in the moment than just going and jumping to a diagram or they aren\'t sure of their indicators at all, which is really a separate problem. You know, maybe that\'s the second biggest\\r\\n- 1:46diagramming habit people have that\'s bad is not really knowing their indicators. But if you let\'s say you do, you still might be not quite comfortable enough with translating them into the real meaning of the phrase\\r\\n- 2:00rather than just like beep beep boop robot. I put thing after arrow, you know, like that, that is like a typically a lesser amount of understanding than actually being able to frame the conceptual relationship between the two variables yourself in a personalized if then before you put the\\r\\n- 2:19diagram down because you really want to understand what the statement that they wrote, what it really means to then know that your conditional is right.\\r\\n- 2:28Very often we have a lot of anxiety about like, what if my conditional is wrong? I don\'t want a diagram because what if I put it down wrong\\r\\n- 2:34and all of that different kind of stuff? And honestly, this whole conceptual Translation step of really feeling out what they mean by the statement they give you, that\'s before you diagram. That\'s the way to know that your conditional is right. Because if you actually translated into a personalized if then\\r\\n- 2:51before you throw the diagram down, then you\'ll know that, OK, did what I write match my personalized if then because I generated my personalized if then Translation on the basis of whatever it is that they wrote.\\r\\n- 3:05And so then you kind of have that intermediary checkpoint phase. And of course, that personalized if then it has to match the meaning of whatever words they gave you in the stimulus\\r\\n- 3:13and it has to match what you put down on the diagram. But it\'s that nice little middle man to just kind of double check each side.\\r\\n- 3:20And so I hope that this helps. I hope that this encourages you to diagram in the quip stage of your Translation sentence by sentence, not putting off your diagram and all the way to the end of the stimulus and then probably declining to do it because it costs too much time.\\r\\n- 3:34It doesn\'t actually cost that much time. It\'s just the same as taking a note. And I hope that it has you actually think about the meaning of the phrases you\'re diagramming before you throw them down on the paper again in that quip stage of the Translation. All right.\\r\\n- 3:47Hope this helped and I\'ll see you soon.\\r\\n\\r\\n##### In This Training Block\\r\\n\\r\\n## Video Course Review\\r\\n\\r\\n[\\r\\n\\r\\nConcept Review\\r\\n\\r\\n](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/concept-review)\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE raw\\\\sources\\\\old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07\\\\Clippings\\\\loophole-online\\\\workouts\\\\Proto Stimulus Rebound • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Proto Stimulus Rebound • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/proto-stimulus-rebound\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-30\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Quick Facts\\r\\n\\r\\n### Will This Help Me?\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will help you if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou forget a significant amount of the stimulus by the time you finish the CLIR.\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip for now if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou retain the all information in the stimulus after you\'ve finished the CLIR.\\r\\n\\r\\nchallenge indexBuckle Up\\r\\n\\r\\nlevelBeginner\\r\\n\\r\\nminimum time25+ minutes\\r\\n\\r\\n![clock](https://new.theloophole.com/_next/static/media/clock.f4c45ff9.svg)\\r\\n\\r\\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n**NOTE**: Proto-Stimulus Rebounds may be done with or without answering the question.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Translate, quip, and CLIR the stimulus\\\\*.\\r\\n2. Cover the stimulus and say the full Translation from memory\\r\\n\\t1. Try to reorder the argument parts in your retelling of the Translation.\\r\\n3. Relook at the stimulus and see if anything is missing, added, or changed.\\r\\n\\t1. Take a note of what was missing, added, or changed.\\r\\n4. Complete the rest of the question, if you choose to do so.\\r\\n\\r\\nSections to use\\r\\n\\r\\nMix in Previously Seen Sections\\r\\n\\r\\nhow to time\\r\\n\\r\\nCount-Up Stopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Expanded Universe\\r\\n\\r\\n### Similar Workouts You\'ll Probably Enjoy\\r\\n\\r\\nWorkout\\r\\n\\r\\n### [Translation + CLIR Drill](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/translation-plus-clir-drill)\\r\\n\\r\\nTransform your reading for LSAT mastery\\r\\n\\r\\nMost Popular\\r\\n\\r\\nMost Repeated\\r\\n\\r\\nMost Improved\\r\\n\\r\\nLR\\r\\n\\r\\n### Turn This Workout Into a Full Routine\\r\\n\\r\\nRoutine, 6 Workouts\\r\\n\\r\\n### [Non-Native Speaker Intermediate Translation Routine](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/routines/non-native-speaker-intermediate-translation-routine)\\r\\n\\r\\nYou can seriously improve your English skills\\r\\n\\r\\n🧱 Daily Foundation\\r\\n\\r\\nLR\\r\\n\\r\\nRC\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE raw\\\\sources\\\\old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07\\\\Clippings\\\\loophole-online\\\\workouts\\\\Decaf RC Translation Drill • LO.md\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: \\"Decaf RC Translation Drill • LO\\"\\r\\nsource: \\"https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/workouts/decaf-rc-translation-drill\\"\\r\\nauthor:\\r\\npublished:\\r\\ncreated: 2026-08-27\\r\\ndescription: \\"LSAT prep from Ellen Cassidy, author of The Loophole in LSAT Logical Reasoning — video lessons, drills, and guided study plans.\\"\\r\\ntags:\\r\\n  - \\"clippings\\"\\r\\n---\\r\\n## Quick Facts\\r\\n\\r\\n### Will This Help Me?\\r\\n\\r\\nThis will help you if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou need to translate an RC passage. (Which is all of you? 😂)\\r\\n\\r\\nSkip for now if\\r\\n\\r\\nYou cannot RTQ a stimulus reliably with good process.\\r\\n\\r\\nchallenge indexBuckle Up\\r\\n\\r\\nlevelBeginner\\r\\n\\r\\nminimum section time32+ minutes\\r\\n\\r\\n![clock](https://new.theloophole.com/_next/static/media/clock.f4c45ff9.svg)\\r\\n\\r\\ntiming compatible withStopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\n**NOTE**: Do your recaps out loud in the beginning to keep yourself honest about the quality and completeness of the recaps. Transition to silent recaps when you feel like you\'re ready.\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 1\\\\*. Do it just like LR.\\r\\n\\t1. This means all the details are included just like they would be on LR.\\r\\n\\t\\t2. Quip after *every* Translation.\\r\\n\\t\\t3. This standard applies to every paragraph you ever translate on RC.\\r\\n2. Look away from paragraph 1 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 1 silently.\\r\\n\\t1. The recap is not a summary, gist, or main point.\\r\\n\\t\\t2. The recap should not omit names, dates, and lists.\\r\\n\\t\\t3. The recap should not be a shopping list of information. It should be a story in your own words.\\r\\n\\t\\t4. It is encouraged to mix up the order of the sentences and ideas.\\r\\n\\t\\t5. This standard applies to every paragraph (or collection of paragraphs) you ever recap on RC.\\r\\n3. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 2. Do it just like LR.\\r\\n4. Look away from paragraph 2 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 2 silently.\\r\\n5. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 back to back silently.\\r\\n6. Read, translate, and quip paragraph 3. Do it just like LR.\\r\\n7. Look away from paragraph 3 and recap the **FULL TRANSLATION** of paragraph 3 silently.\\r\\n8. Without looking back, recap the Translations of paragraphs 1 + 2 + 3 back to back silently.\\r\\n9. Repeat this pattern for all remaining paragraphs.\\r\\n10. Complete this same process on all remaining passages in the section.\\r\\n\\r\\nvideo walkthrough\\r\\n\\r\\n[How and Why to Do Decaf RC Translation](https://new.theloophole.com/training-camp/browse/stage/translation/video/how-and-why-to-do-decaf-rc-translation)\\r\\n\\r\\nSections to use\\r\\n\\r\\nMix in Previously Seen Sections\\r\\n\\r\\nhow to time\\r\\n\\r\\nCount-Up Stopwatch\\r\\n\\r\\nper passage time goal to move on\\r\\n\\r\\n12 min (outloud recaps), 8 min (silent recaps)\\r\\n\\r\\n## Ellen\'s Advice\\r\\n\\r\\n### If You Want to Win\\r\\n\\r\\n- If you have a 70-minute time accommodation, you can use Decaf RC Translation on test day. You do not have to master Cappuccino RC Translation and Caffeinated RC Translation for use on test day, if Decaf RC Translation offers superior Translation retention.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The Expanded Universe\\r\\n\\r\\n### Videos to Supercharge Your Workout\\r\\n\\r\\n### Get Help in These Training Blocks\\r\\n\\r\\n1\\r\\n\\r\\n### Turn This Workout Into a Full Routine\\r\\n\\r\\nQuick Routine, 3 Workouts\\r\\n\\r\\n### [The RC Working Memory Workout Routine](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/routines/the-rc-working-memory-workout-routine)\\r\\n\\r\\nYou. will. remember. the. passage.\\r\\n\\r\\n🧱 Daily Foundation\\r\\n\\r\\nLR\\r\\n\\r\\nRC\\r\\n\\r\\nQuick Routine, 6 Workouts\\r\\n\\r\\n### [AC Primer + Decaf Routine](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/routines/ac-primer-plus-decaf-routine)\\r\\n\\r\\nRemember the passage and answer choices for real\\r\\n\\r\\n🧱 Daily Foundation\\r\\n\\r\\nLR\\r\\n\\r\\nRC\\r\\n\\r\\nRoutine, 6 Workouts\\r\\n\\r\\n### [Decaf + LR Sections Routine](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/routines/decaf-plus-lr-sections-routine)\\r\\n\\r\\nBuild the rock solid foundation your goal score needs\\r\\n\\r\\n🧱 Daily Foundation\\r\\n\\r\\nMost Repeated\\r\\n\\r\\nTop Performing\\r\\n\\r\\nLR\\r\\n\\r\\nRC\\r\\n\\r\\nQuick Routine, 3 Workouts\\r\\n\\r\\n### [The RC Get to Know You Routine](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/routines/the-rc-get-to-know-you-routine)\\r\\n\\r\\nRC can be breezy and light\\r\\n\\r\\n🧱 Daily Foundation\\r\\n\\r\\nRC\\r\\n\\r\\nRoutine, 5 Workouts\\r\\n\\r\\n### [Non-Native Speaker Advanced Beginner Translation Routine](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/routines/non-native-speaker-advanced-beginner-translation-routine)\\r\\n\\r\\nStay patient with yourself; you\'re doing something great here\\r\\n\\r\\n🧱 Daily Foundation\\r\\n\\r\\nLR\\r\\n\\r\\nRC\\r\\n\\r\\nRoutine, 5 Workouts\\r\\n\\r\\n### [Translation + Decaf Routine](https://new.theloophole.com/workouts-and-routines/routines/translation-plus-decaf-routine)\\r\\n\\r\\nThis might be the hardest Routine you do in Loophole Online\\r\\n\\r\\n🧱 Daily Foundation\\r\\n\\r\\nCommunity Favorite\\r\\n\\r\\nMost Repeated\\r\\n\\r\\nLR\\r\\n\\r\\nRC\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE raw\\\\sources\\\\old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07\\\\Assumptions & The Objection.md\\r\\n# Chapter 5 — Assumptions & The Objection\\r\\n\\r\\n*Transcribed from the quiz screenshots in [[Untitled 3.md]]. \\"Loophole\\" is rendered throughout as \\"Objection\\"; everything else is verbatim. Questions 1, 2, and 6 were not captured.*\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 3\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith are kitten best friends who routinely engage in mischievous conduct spanning everything from play-wrestling to napping on radiators. As a pair, they have already gnawed through half a cigar, gotten trapped under an area rug, and claimed the counter as their own. Therefore, Hoodoo and Keith knocked over a vase of daffodils.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is not an Objection in the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if kitten best friends aren\'t strong enough to knock over a vase?\\r\\n- **B. What if knocking over a vase of daffodils doesn\'t qualify as mischievous conduct?** ✅\\r\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith have never had access to a vase of daffodils?\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 4\\r\\n\\r\\nHow do you design an Objection?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if the conclusion is not true?\\r\\n- B. What if one of the premises is not really true?\\r\\n- **C. What if [really bad thing for the argument]?** ✅\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 5\\r\\n\\r\\nHow will you know if your Objection works?\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A. If the Objection is possible, the conclusion is no longer proven.** ✅\\r\\n- B. The Objection works as a valid prephrase.\\r\\n- C. The Objection addresses key concepts in the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 7\\r\\n\\r\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if Keith won\'t hide?\\r\\n- **B. What if Keith doesn\'t hide when he\'s stressed?** ✅\\r\\n- C. What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 8 — explanation\\r\\n\\r\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\r\\n\\r\\n*What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?*\\r\\nThe premises told us that Keith is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house. We can\'t negate the premises.\\r\\n\\r\\n*What if Keith won\'t hide?*\\r\\nThe conclusion claims that Keith will hide. We can\'t just negate the conclusion and call it an Objection.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 9\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?\\r\\n- B. What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?\\r\\n- **C. What if Hoodoo and Keith are not representative of all felines?** ✅\\r\\n\\r\\n## Question 10 — explanation\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\r\\n\\r\\n*What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?*\\r\\nThe argument only assumes that Hoodoo and Keith \\"respond\\" to language, not that they understand language. Pointing out that Hoodoo and Keith may not understand language does not affect the validity of the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n*What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?*\\r\\nHoodoo and Keith becoming more like wild cats does not affect whether or not we can generalize from their behaviors to those of all felines.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE raw\\\\sources\\\\old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07\\\\Objection Mini-Quiz.md\\r\\n# Objection — Mini-Quiz\\r\\n\\r\\n5 questions. Pick one answer each. Answer key at the bottom.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith are kitten best friends who routinely engage in mischievous conduct spanning everything from play-wrestling to napping on radiators. As a pair, they have already gnawed through half a cigar, gotten trapped under an area rug, and claimed the counter as their own. Therefore, Hoodoo and Keith knocked over a vase of daffodils.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is **not** an Objection in the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if kitten best friends aren\'t strong enough to knock over a vase?\\r\\n- B. What if knocking over a vase of daffodils doesn\'t qualify as mischievous conduct?\\r\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith have never had access to a vase of daffodils?\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2\\r\\n\\r\\nHow do you design an Objection?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if the conclusion is not true?\\r\\n- B. What if one of the premises is not really true?\\r\\n- C. What if [really bad thing for the argument]?\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3\\r\\n\\r\\nHow will you know if your Objection works?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. If the Objection is possible, the conclusion is no longer proven.\\r\\n- B. The Objection works as a valid prephrase.\\r\\n- C. The Objection addresses key concepts in the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 4\\r\\n\\r\\n*Keith is afraid of new people and gets stressed in new environments. He is spending the weekend at his aunt\'s house for the first time, so he will hide all weekend.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if Keith won\'t hide?\\r\\n- B. What if Keith doesn\'t hide when he\'s stressed?\\r\\n- C. What if Keith\'s aunt cancels their weekend plans?\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### 5\\r\\n\\r\\n*Hoodoo and Keith love to watch reality TV and paw at the speaker when people speak. This proves that all felines do in fact respond to language, since Hoodoo and Keith are house cats.*\\r\\n\\r\\nWhich one of the following is an Objection for the argument above?\\r\\n\\r\\n- A. What if Hoodoo and Keith don\'t really understand language but instead are simply responding to the sounds of reality TV?\\r\\n- B. What if Hoodoo and Keith are becoming more like wild cats?\\r\\n- C. What if Hoodoo and Keith are not representative of all felines?\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Answer key\\r\\n\\r\\n**1. B** — the other two attack the argument; this one doesn\'t.\\r\\n\\r\\n**2. C** — you don\'t negate the conclusion or the premises. You ask what would wreck the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n**3. A** — if the Objection is possible and the conclusion no longer follows, it works.\\r\\n\\r\\n**4. B** — A negates the conclusion, C negates a premise. B breaks the link between stress and hiding.\\r\\n\\r\\n**5. C** — A: the argument only claims they respond, not that they understand. B: turning into wild cats doesn\'t touch whether you can generalize. C: kills the generalization from two house cats to all felines.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/paraphrase-before-diagramming.md\n+---\n+type: teaching-notes\n+subject: Understand a conditional before diagramming it\n+created: 2026-10-07\n+updated: 2026-10-07\n+aliases: []\n+tags: [lsat, conditionals, teacher-reference]\n+sources:\n+  - ../../../../wiki/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07.md\n+---\n+\n+# Check the meaning before accepting the diagram\n+\n+Private teacher method recovered from Ellen Cassidy's “The Worst Conditional Habit Everyone Has,” captured 2026-08-30. It supplements the existing [conditional reference](_megadoc_reference_conditionals.md) and [Joshua-selected teaching voice standard](../../../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md). It is not a new published lesson or a replacement indicator system.\n+\n+## Use an if-then paraphrase as the middle check\n+\n+Ask the student to explain the relationship in an if-then sentence before drawing the arrow. Check the paraphrase against the original wording. Then check the diagram against that paraphrase.\n+\n+A correct-looking arrow is insufficient evidence of understanding if the student cannot explain what triggers the rule and what the rule requires. Conversely, verbal confidence does not establish a correct diagram. The two representations should agree.\n+\n+For example, “Only members received ballots” means that anyone who received a ballot was a member: `received ballot → member`. Being a member alone does not establish that someone received a ballot. This example is an original illustration of the recovered method, not a quotation from the clip.\n+\n+## Diagnose the disagreement\n+\n+- If the paraphrase changes the original meaning, revisit the wording and indicator before manipulating symbols.\n+- If the paraphrase is accurate but the arrow reverses it, practise matching the diagram to the explained relationship.\n+- If both are accurate but the inference reverses the rule, revisit legal inference and contraposition using the existing curriculum.\n+\n+Do not infer a student has one of these problems without hearing their explanation. Record the actual observed error in their teaching notes, then link this reusable method rather than copying it into a parallel student lesson.\n+\n+Cassidy places diagramming during the content-reaction stage of RTQ. That placement and her claims about speed remain source prescriptions; the useful recovered check is agreement among text, paraphrase and diagram. [Preserved clip](<../../../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/training-camp/learn/The Worst Conditional Habit Everyone Has • LO.md>); [method collection](../../../loophole-online/index.md); [diagnosis framework](../../../tutoring-diagnosis.md).\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/Curriculum/Reading Comprehension/Notes/cumulative-translation-review.md\n+---\n+type: teaching-notes\n+subject: Check whether earlier RC paragraphs survive later reading\n+created: 2026-10-07\n+updated: 2026-10-07\n+aliases: []\n+tags: [lsat, reading-comprehension, teacher-reference]\n+sources:\n+  - ../../../../wiki/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07.md\n+---\n+\n+# Check whether earlier paragraphs survive later reading\n+\n+Private teacher method recovered from Ellen Cassidy's Decaf RC workout, captured 2026-08-27. Use when observed recall loses earlier details as a passage continues. This is a practice protocol, not a universal timed-test prescription or evidence that any particular student needs it.\n+\n+## Check recall cumulatively\n+\n+Have the student read a paragraph, restate its information in their own words and react to its specific content. Looking away, they retell that paragraph. After the second paragraph, they retell it and then the first two together. Continue the cumulative retelling as later paragraphs are added.\n+\n+The source's target is a full translation, including relevant names, dates, lists and qualifiers. A gist or main point is a different output. Keep that distinction explicit when checking the exercise; success on a gist task does not establish success on a detailed-recall task.\n+\n+Start aloud so the teacher can hear omissions, additions and changed relationships. Check those against the passage, and record the actual discrepancy. The source later permits silent practice, but silent confidence alone does not show the teacher what was retained.\n+\n+## Choose it for an observed reading problem\n+\n+Separate losing the passage from misunderstanding a question task or running out of time earlier in the section. The [tutoring diagnosis framework](../../../tutoring-diagnosis.md) explains those distinct hypotheses. For LR recall after analysis, the related Proto-Stimulus Rebound protocol retells the stimulus after CLIR, checks missing/added/changed information, and records the discrepancy; see the [method collection](../../../loophole-online/index.md).\n+\n+Cassidy's stated workload, 12-minute aloud/8-minute silent goals and advice for 70-minute accommodations are historical vendor recommendations. They were not adopted as Joshua's timing targets or assigned to anyone. Follow [Joshua's teaching voice standard](../../../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md) when turning evidence from this method into a student-facing explanation.\n+\n+[Preserved Decaf workout](<../../../../raw/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07/Clippings/loophole-online/workouts/Decaf RC Translation Drill • LO.md>); [source summary](../../../../wiki/sources/old-obsidian-vault-2026-10-07.md); [corpus index](../../../index.md).\n*** Update File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/Curriculum/Flaw and Famous Flaws/Homework/Pop Quiz - Assumption vs Objection.md\n@@\n *Source: Google Doc\n*** End Patch"));

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.2 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"c1c1b9","wall_time_seconds":0.2232738,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":569,"output":"---\\r\\nname: lsat-u-voice\\r\\ndescription: Draft, revise, or calibrate LSAT teaching material using Joshua\'s selected voice standard. Use for lessons, notes, explanations, drills, quizzes, flashcards, and instructional feedback. Logistics emails and marketing use their separate canonical registers.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Write LSAT material in Joshua\'s selected teaching voice\\r\\n\\r\\nResolve the canonical ai-os vault from the workspace instructions. This project-owned skill takes precedence over older vendor skills with the same name that prescribe a writer blend.\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore drafting or revising, read:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. [Canonical teaching voice](../../../wiki/teaching-voice.md), especially the teaching-content register.\\r\\n2. [LSAT teaching voice standard](../../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md). Read it in full when calibrating the voice; for subsequent revisions, revisit the relevant sections and examples.\\r\\n3. [Demonstrated copy corrections](../../../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).\\r\\n\\r\\nThe detailed standard is maintained in one place. Do not copy it into this skill or replace it with generic brevity rules, required catchphrases, humor quotas, or a fixed number of sentences per paragraph.\\r\\n\\r\\nEstablish the actual lesson topic, prerequisites, evidence, and Joshua\'s approved method. The conditional reference teaches how to explain; it does not add conditional content to another topic. Preserve complete ideas, logical strength, modality, and the selected scope.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse original intuitive examples. Explain the ordinary meaning before notation, show the intermediate reasoning, and give questions a real instructional job. Keep the teacher and reader thinking together. When useful, show a tempting interpretation and how its meaning fails. Fresh practice should require the taught skill and provide reasoned feedback.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead the draft aloud, check its inferences and boundaries, and apply the standard\'s review rubric. Review actual prompt/reveal states when writing for an interactive surface. Do not claim an identical voice has been objectively established; use Joshua\'s corrections to refine calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis skill governs local authoring. Posting, sending, assigning, deploying, or generating paid media requires the authorization and workflow appropriate to that action.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a266a6","wall_time_seconds":0.2182204,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2488,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: synthesis\\r\\nsubject: LSAT teaching voice standard\\r\\ncreated: 2026-10-02\\r\\nupdated: 2026-10-03\\r\\naliases: [LSAT voice reference, Conditional transcription writing standard]\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, writing]\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - ../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT teaching: make the reasoning sound like someone thinking\\r\\n\\r\\n**This is Joshua\'s selected reference for writing LSAT teaching material.** Use it for bootcamp lessons, lecture notes, explanations, guided examples, drills, flashcards, quizzes, reference pages, and instructional feedback. It develops the teaching-content register in [the canonical teaching voice](../wiki/teaching-voice.md). That document still governs correspondence and marketing in their own registers.\\r\\n\\r\\nJoshua selected the supplied **Conditional Reasoning** transcription on 2026-10-02 because of its writing, tone, word choice, cadence, point of view, progression, and depth. The goal is to reproduce those qualities closely through original wording and examples. The central quality is patient, conversational reasoning that the reader can follow and eventually perform.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is a calibration example. This guide separates **observed features** from **instructions for our material**. A source habit is not automatically a rule, a research finding, or permission to import a topic. Matching the voice does not require repeating the same metaphors, anecdotes, exercise sentences, or personal claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Find the part you need\\r\\n\\r\\n- [The evidence and coverage](#the-evidence-and-coverage)\\r\\n- [The voice in one original passage](#the-voice-in-one-original-passage)\\r\\n- [How the teacher relates to the reader](#how-the-teacher-relates-to-the-reader)\\r\\n- [Sentence length and spoken cadence](#sentence-length-and-spoken-cadence)\\r\\n- [Word choice and useful verbal habits](#word-choice-and-useful-verbal-habits)\\r\\n- [Questions that make the reader think](#questions-that-make-the-reader-think)\\r\\n- [Transitions, repetition, metaphor, and emphasis](#transitions-explain-why-the-next-move-follows)\\r\\n- [The complete source progression and depth](#the-complete-source-progression-and-depth)\\r\\n- [What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp](#what-to-carry-into-the-conditional-bootcamp)\\r\\n- [Accuracy and method boundaries](#accuracy-and-method-boundaries)\\r\\n- [Calibration examples and review](#calibration-examples-for-different-lsat-surfaces)\\r\\n- [A reusable writing brief](#a-reusable-writing-brief)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The evidence and coverage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe [source summary](../wiki/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice.md) identifies the [preserved HTML](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html), [provenance](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/PROVENANCE.md), and SHA-256. Every supplied section, FAQ entry, table, worked diagram, and drill item was read. HTML line references below refer to that unchanged archived file.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe document has six main sections and six subordinate headings. It ends with six basic drill items and an answer-key reference. One line is explicitly marked partially obscured. The answer key is not supplied. This analysis covers the entire attachment; it cannot reconstruct the missing line or establish the contents of the larger chapter.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe HTML says its formatting was adjusted. We can examine what a pause, table, side note, or separated example does for learning. We cannot claim its font, spacing, color, or CSS exactly reproduces the original publication. No author is identified in the file. The exercise wording attributed to Shakespeare is separate from the narrator\'s teaching voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The voice in one original passage\\r\\n\\r\\nThe following is a new calibration passage. Its example was created for this guide; it is not a source quotation or an approved bootcamp page.\\r\\n\\r\\n> A conditional tells us that if one thing is true, something else has to be true too.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Suppose a shop has this rule: **Every parcel with a blue sticker has been inspected.**\\r\\n>\\r\\n> You pick up a parcel with a blue sticker. What do you know about it?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> It has been inspected. The rule covers every parcel with that sticker, including the one in your hand. A blue-sticker parcel that hadn\'t been inspected would break the rule.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Now turn the parcel over. It has an inspection stamp, but you can\'t see a blue sticker. Does it have to have one?\\r\\n>\\r\\n> We don\'t know. The rule tells us something about parcels with blue stickers. It doesn\'t tell us that every inspected parcel gets a sticker.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> That\'s the distinction we need: **what the sentence makes certain, and what it leaves open.**\\r\\n\\r\\nNotice the opening definition before the example, the small prediction, the explanation connecting the rule to the case, and the change in what the reader is given. The last sentence names a distinction the reader has just seen. The passage has a conversational rhythm because it follows a person\'s thoughts. Casual vocabulary alone would not accomplish that.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn an interactive lesson, pause after the first question and again after the inspection-stamp question. Reveal each explanation after the learner has had an opportunity to answer. The printed passage shows the whole progression for calibration.\\r\\n\\r\\n## How the teacher relates to the reader\\r\\n\\r\\n### The reader is beside the teacher\\r\\n\\r\\nThe narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let\'s,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you\'re a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Confidence lives in the reasoning\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don\'t know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Warmth points at the work\\r\\n\\r\\nEncouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.\\r\\n\\r\\nUseful form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.\\r\\n\\r\\nWeak form:\\r\\n\\r\\n> You\'ve got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!\\r\\n\\r\\nThe weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.\\r\\n\\r\\n### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |\\r\\n| **We / let\'s** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let\'s keep the rule and change the fact.” |\\r\\n| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |\\r\\n| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I\'m taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |\\r\\n| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator\'s student history. |\\r\\n\\r\\nKeep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sentence length and spoken cadence\\r\\n\\r\\n### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain\\r\\n\\r\\nThe source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That\'s it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal rhythm:\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a8c3b8","wall_time_seconds":0.194229,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2,"output":"False\\r\\n"}'}]
